Create a top-down architectural plan of ELODIN'S NAMING LECTURE HALL
inside Mains, from the Kingkiller Chronicle as a canon-constrained
reconstruction. This is a single-room plan, not a whole-floor plan —
the drawing should show this room, its immediate threshold doors, and
a short stub of the Mains hallway outside it. Nothing more.

Canon (must not contradict):
- The room is in MAINS, not Hollows. Specifically: "the lecture hall
  in Mains where Elodin's class was being held" (WMF Ch 13). Mains
  is the oldest building at the University; its hallways take odd
  turns and dead-end unexpectedly (NOTW Ch 38). This room is one of
  several lecture halls inside Mains — Hemme's room is also a Mains
  lecture hall but distinctly smaller (NOTW Ch 38 — "small theater,"
  tiered semicircles around a small raised stage, ~50-seat
  capacity).
- Elodin's hall is HUGE and feels oversized for its current use:
  "the huge, empty room" (WMF Ch 13); "hollow and lifeless with only
  a handful of students" (WMF Ch 44 area). It was built for the old
  University where naming was the central discipline; now it holds
  six or seven students at most.
- Access is from a HALLWAY INSIDE MAINS. Students wait in the
  hallway before class begins; the door opens off that hallway
  (WMF Ch 13). The door can stick and seem locked when it is only
  jammed.
- The entry door enters the room at the TOP / BACK of the
  auditorium. Students "walked down the stairs to the front row of
  seats" (WMF Ch 13) — the room descends away from the entry toward
  the lecturer at the front.
- SEATING is tiered stone, with stepped rows descending toward the
  front (WMF Ch 13 — stairs down; WMF Ch 15 — Elodin trips and
  falls painfully on the "stone floor"). Multiple rows; canonical
  count not given. The room is large enough that Elodin can run
  around chasing floating milkweed seeds for roughly ten minutes
  without reaching its limits (WMF Ch 15).
- At the FRONT of the room:
  - A LECTURER'S DAIS (WMF Ch 15: Elodin "ran across the lecturer's
    dais"; WMF Ch 44 area: "climbed onto the dais and moved to stand
    behind the lectern").
  - A LECTERN on the dais (WMF Ch 44 area). Elodin almost never
    uses it; its presence is architectural and institutional.
  - A LARGE SLATE mounted at the front of the room, written on with
    chalk (WMF Ch 13 — "On the large slate in front of us, written
    in Elodin's oddly tidy handwriting"; WMF Ch 15 context).
  - A TABLE on or near the dais (WMF Ch 13 — Elodin "dropped them
    onto a table"; WMF Ch 15 — "jumped onto the table at the front
    of the room").
  - CHAIRS at the front (WMF Ch 15 — Elodin "clambered over chairs"
    and eventually "tripped over a chair and tumbled painfully to
    the stone floor").
- HIGH CEILING. Milkweed seeds thrown upward sail "toward the high
  ceiling of the lecture hall" (WMF Ch 15). The room's vertical
  volume is notably larger than its floor plan would suggest.
- Construction is STONE — stone floor, stone seating, stone walls.
  Consistent with Mains generally.

Reconstructed programme (flag clearly; do not render as canon):
- A MASTER'S / LECTURER'S LOWER DOOR giving direct access onto the
  dais from behind, bypassing the auditorium. Canon does not specify
  one for this room but Hemme's Mains lecture hall has exactly this
  feature (NOTW Ch 39: "entered by the hall's lower door and climbed
  the three steps onto the raised wooden stage"). Parallel reading
  from the same building supports placing one here too. Label it
  reconstructed.
- A narrow service / staff corridor behind the dais connecting the
  lower door to the wider Mains circulation. Implied by the lower
  door; reconstructed.
- The exact SEATING SHAPE — straight rows, curved rows, or tiered
  semicircles. Canon does not specify. Hemme's smaller Mains hall
  uses "tiered semicircles" (NOTW Ch 38); parallel reading supports
  tiered semicircles here too, scaled up. Label reconstructed.
- Row count and total seat capacity. Canon says "huge" and "empty
  with a handful of students." Draw somewhere between 150 and 250
  seats — large enough to justify "huge," small enough to stay
  honest. Flag the count as reconstructed.
- Dais step height and stage depth. Hemme's hall has a three-step
  rise to its stage (NOTW Ch 39); reasonable parallel here.
- The door's exact mechanism — a sticky latch that can be confused
  with a locked door (WMF Ch 13). Render as a standard wooden door
  in plan; do not invent hardware detail.

Position inside Mains (canon + implied):
- Mains is a sprawling, acre-and-a-half composite building with
  irrational internal geography (NOTW Ch 38). The lecture hall can
  sit anywhere in the building shell that the approved Mains plan
  allows.
- Do NOT put it adjacent to Hemme's lecture hall on this plan —
  canon implies the lecture halls are distributed across Mains, not
  clustered. Leave the broader Mains floor plan to its own sheet.

Hard bans:
- no siting in Hollows — Elodin has an OFFICE in Hollows (NOTW
  Ch 46) but TEACHES in Mains (WMF Ch 13). The huge-room-with-slate
  is Mains only.
- no windows positioned to create "stage lighting" or dramatic
  spotlight effects — this is a working lecture hall, not a theatre.
  If windows are shown, keep them high on exterior walls, plain
  leaded glass, consistent with Mains generally. Windows may be
  omitted entirely — the room can plausibly be an interior hall
  lit by sympathy lamps, especially on a lower floor.
- no stained glass — that is Hollows (Teccam on the front face,
  NOTW Ch 36).
- no crescent table, raised masters' dais, or circle-of-light
  staging — those belong to the Admissions Theatre in Hollows
  (NOTW Ch 36). A lectern and a slate are the correct front-of-room
  fittings.
- no proscenium arch, theatre curtain, stage flats, or performance
  hall decoration — this is an academic teaching space.
- no courtroom dock, witness box, or jury benches — trials happen
  in the Masters' Hall in Hollows (NOTW Ch 40).
- no fixed desk writing surfaces built into the seating — canon
  shows students bringing their own hardbacks and loose papers
  (WMF Ch 13: Elodin "dropped them onto a table"; NOTW Ch 38:
  students arrive with "hardbacks to write on" and "wax tablets").
  Seats are seats.
- no single-level flat-floor classroom — canon requires a TIERED
  stepped descent from the entry door to the front.
- no entry door at the FRONT of the room at floor level — canon
  explicitly has students entering at the top and walking DOWN to
  the front. A lower door for the master is separate and
  reconstructed.
- no cathedral-like vaulted fan ribbing or gothic ornament — the
  high ceiling is plain stone; the effect is austere scale, not
  ornament.

Drawing style:
architectural floor plan
black and white
clear walls, doors, stairs, steps, thresholds
seating drawn as tiered rows with stepped contours
dais and stage clearly marked at the front with elevation change
lectern, table, chairs, and wall-mounted slate drawn as plan
  symbols (these are architecturally relevant — draw them)
circulation arrows or dashed lines for student entry (top/back)
  and master entry (reconstructed lower door)
room label
north arrow (consistent with the master campus map and the
  approved Mains floor plan)
scale bar
no people
no decorative fantasy elements

Rules:
- Respect the TOP-TO-BOTTOM descent: entry at the back / top;
  tiered stone seating stepping down; lecturer's dais with slate at
  the front / bottom.
- Respect the OVERSIZED scale: the room must read as much larger
  than its current enrolment needs. This is an old-University naming
  hall, built when the discipline was central.
- Keep fittings minimal at the front: a dais, a lectern, a table,
  a handful of chairs, a wall-mounted slate. Nothing more.
- Do not populate the hall with furniture or ornament beyond what
  canon confirms.
- Label each element canon, implied, or reconstructed in a
  visible map key.

Consistency requirements:
- Keep north aligned with the master campus map and with the
  approved Mains floor plan.
- Room footprint, wall positions, and door positions must match the
  lecture hall as it appears (or will appear) on the approved Mains
  floor plan.
- Stepped seating and the dais elevation change must be internally
  consistent — a ceiling high enough to accommodate both a steep
  descent and genuine vertical volume above the dais.
- Do not contradict earlier approved plans for Mains, Hollows, or
  the master campus.
- If a new detail would force a contradiction, reject the detail
  rather than improvising.

Create a top-down architectural site plan of the University grounds from the Kingkiller Chronicle as a canon-constrained reconstruction, not a fantasy invention.

Style:
clean black-and-white planning drawing
architectural site plan
north arrow
simple labels
no decorative fantasy art
no characters

Required constraints:
## 1. What is explicitly known
- Subterranean network beneath the University
- Connected to Archives via ventilation tunnels
- Contains multiple zones and levels
- Accessed through various building connections
- Complex underground system with multiple areas

## 2. What is strongly implied
- Extensive subterranean infrastructure
- Ventilation and utility connections
- Multiple access points from surface buildings
- Complex network of tunnels and chambers
- Utility and maintenance functions

## 3. What is weakly inferred
- Stone construction with underground modifications
- Multiple levels and chambers
- Ventilation systems connecting to surface
- Utility corridors and service areas
- Emergency and maintenance access

## 4. What must not be contradicted
- No surface-level presence
- No disconnection from Archives
- No lack of multiple zones
- No absence of ventilation connections
- No simple single tunnel system

## 5. Likely building function and circulation
The Underthing serves as subterranean utility and ventilation network beneath the University, functioning as infrastructure hub connecting surface buildings. Circulation includes tunnel networks, ventilation systems, and service corridors with complex multi-level organization.

## 6. Likely entrances, stairs, and room zones
- Access points from surface buildings (Archives)
- Ventilation tunnel connections
- Multiple underground zones and levels
- Utility and service corridors
- Maintenance and access chambers
- Emergency egress routes

## 7. Unknowns that must remain unknown
- Exact tunnel configurations
- Total number of zones and levels
- Specific building connections
- Ventilation system details
- Utility exact functions
- Complete underground extent

## 8. Final reconstruction summary
An extensive subterranean network beneath the University with multiple zones and levels, connected to surface buildings through ventilation tunnels. The underground system serves utility and infrastructure functions with complex tunnel networks, service corridors, and maintenance access points throughout the campus.

  canon

Create a top-down architectural plan of LEDGERS AND LISTS, a single
room on the ground floor of Hollows, from the Kingkiller Chronicle
as a canon-constrained reconstruction. This is a single-room plan,
not a whole-floor plan — the drawing should show this room and its
threshold door onto the Hollows ground-floor circulation, nothing
more.

Canon (must not contradict):
- Ledgers and Lists is the University's registrar function. Named
  three times in canon, always in the same registrar-register:
  - NOTW Ch 46 — Kvothe visits to look up what classes Elodin
    teaches. The ledger lists each class by NAME, by TIME, and by
    LOCATION. In Elodin's case the entry reads "now" / "everywhere,"
    which is canonically unusual but structurally consistent: the
    ledger has a Name field, a Time field, and a Location field.
  - WMF Ch 11 — Fela asks whether Elodin's enigmatic class name
    will be listed "under 'The Name of the Class.'" This confirms
    the ledger is organised as a lookup index by class name.
  - WMF Ch 14 — Kvothe signs up for the coming term's classes
    here. Enrolment for the term is a public service the room
    provides.
- Ledgers and Lists is NEVER described as a building, NEVER given a
  location in canon, and NEVER physically described. It appears in
  canon as a function, not as architecture. Everything about the
  room's layout is reconstruction. The map key must reflect this.
- The University has "about fifteen buildings" (NOTW Ch 36), and
  every one of them is named in canon. Ledgers and Lists is not in
  that list, which is why the canon-honest reading is that it is a
  ROOM inside an existing building, not a sixteenth building.
- Building placement: LEDGERS AND LISTS IS LOCATED ON THE GROUND
  FLOOR OF HOLLOWS. Canon does not state this, but it is the
  cleanest reading: Hollows houses the Masters' Hall, master
  offices, Elodin's office, and the Bursar in the basement — every
  administrative function of the University. Co-location follows.
  This placement must be flagged as reconstructed in the key.

Room programme (implied and reconstructed; flag clearly):
- PUBLIC COUNTER. A wooden counter separates the public floor from
  the clerk's workspace. Students approach the counter to look up
  class listings or to enrol for the coming term. Reconstructed.
- LEDGER SHELVING behind the counter. Long open shelving holding
  many bound ledgers. The canonical "ledgers and lists" of the
  room's name — class schedules by term, master teaching
  assignments, student enrolment records by term. Reconstructed as
  to exact layout; canonical in the sense that ledgers must
  physically exist and must be at hand to the clerk.
- CLERK'S WORKSPACE behind the counter. A desk, pen, ink, quill,
  and a fresh ledger on which current-term enrolments are recorded.
  Plausibly one or two clerks at peak times (admissions-week
  volume); fewer the rest of the term. Reconstructed.
- A BACK ROOM for older ledger storage. Previous terms' enrolment
  records, historical class listings, archived teaching
  assignments. Plain shelving; plain stone walls. Reconstructed.
- A small STANDING / QUEUING AREA in front of the counter for
  students waiting to be helped. During admissions week
  (canonically the end of each term — WMF Ch 14) the queue is
  substantial; most of the year it would be near-empty.
  Reconstructed.
- LIGHTING is sympathy lamps only (standard University interior

  room's function.

  reconstructed.
- The back room is reached from behind the counter, not from the

- no treating Ledgers and Lists as a separate building — it is a

FLOOR IDENTITY
This floor should read as a busy institutional medical level serving both University patients and townspeople, with more order than Mains but without false symmetry.

LOCKED CANON
The Medica is a large, oddly shaped building.
It is taller and less rambling than Mains.
It treats more than just students; town patients matter too.
STRONG IMPLICATIONS

Quieter recovery space should sit deeper or farther from the main receiving point.

Put treatment rooms along exterior walls where conservative windows can be justified.
Put a larger recovery ward or quieter convalescent rooms on a calmer exterior edge.
Keep staff workroom, storage, or dispensary near the center of operations rather than at the farthest end.
FIXED ELEMENTS AND FURNITURE

Windows are allowed only on true exterior walls and should be used conservatively.

Ok audit this prompt for the masters residence building please / masters hall to make it as lore accurate as possible please

You’re right to zero in on this — fixing Masters’ Hall cleanly will stabilise a lot of downstream consistency.

Here’s a **high-fidelity, canon-aligned generation prompt** you can drop straight into Bolt / Midjourney / SD / any architectural generator. I’ve baked in the WMF corrections and explicitly avoided the earlier Hollows bleed.

---

## **AI Generation Prompt — Masters’ Hall (Canon-Corrected)**

**Title:**
Masters’ Hall — University Residential Building (Kingkiller Chronicle, WMF-aligned)

**Prompt:**
A realistic architectural visualization of *Masters’ Hall*, a standalone medieval academic residential building at a prestigious arcane university.

The structure is **built of grey stone**, appearing slightly **silvery in moonlight**, with a sober, dignified, and restrained design — not ornate, but authoritative and old. The building is **multi-storey (at least 3–4 levels)** with a **pitched roof** that is physically accessible (implied by students climbing onto it). The stonework is weathered but well-maintained, conveying age, prestige, and quiet importance.

The building’s purpose is **residential and private**, not academic:

* Contains **living quarters for masters**
* **Rooms for resident gillers**
* **Guest rooms for visiting arcanists**
* No classrooms, no lecture halls, no admissions spaces

**Exterior details:**

* A prominent **stained glass window depicting Teccam**:

  * Teccam is barefoot, standing at the mouth of a cave
  * He is addressing a group of students
  * The window glows softly from interior light at night
* **Narrow vertical windows** elsewhere, practical rather than decorative
* A **solid side entrance** with a **brass lock**
* Minimal ornamentation; design emphasizes **authority, privacy, and age**
* Subtle academic atmosphere rather than religious or noble

**Lighting & mood:**

* Time: **night**
* A **single dim interior light** illuminates the stained glass window from within
* Exterior lit by **soft moonlight**, giving the stone a faint metallic sheen
* Overall tone: **quiet, contemplative, slightly austere**

**Surroundings:**

* Located within a larger university complex
* Nearby structures exist but are not the focus
* Grounds are subdued: stone paths, sparse greenery, academic stillness

**Interior hints (optional if cutaway or cross-section is generated):**

* **Narrow stone hallways**
* **Long, narrow staircase leading upward**
* Modest but respectable private rooms
* Functional layout, not grand halls

**Style:**

* Realistic, grounded medieval architecture (not fantasy exaggeration)
* Historically plausible materials and construction
* Subtle fantasy tone, not magical spectacle

---

## **Negative Prompt (important for consistency)**

* No large lecture halls
* No amphitheatres
* No crescent tables or tribunal layouts
* No admissions scenes
* No grand magical effects
* No cathedral-scale ornamentation
* No placement inside another building

---

## **Optional Variant Tags**

If your generator supports style modifiers:

* *“realistic medieval university architecture”*
* *“oxbridge inspired stone college”*
* *“moody night lighting, cinematic, soft glow”*
* *“architectural visualization, high detail, physically accurate materials”*

---

## Why this fixes your canon issue

This prompt enforces:

* Masters’ Hall = **separate building**
* Function = **residential only**
* Teccam window = **here, not Hollows**
* Tone = **quiet authority, not procedural drama**

It also avoids the NOTW-era “trial chamber” interpretation entirely, which is what caused the contamination earlier.

Thought for 1m 11s
Good catch. This prompt is partly right, but still overstates several things.

The strongest canon anchors for Masters’ Hall are:

it is one of the few buildings Kvothe hasn’t spent much time in,

it contains little more than living quarters for the masters, resident gillers, and guest rooms for visiting arcanists,

Elodin approaches a side door and unlocks it with a bright brass key,

inside is a narrow stone hallway and a long, narrow flight of stairs,

later, at night, Kvothe sees the grey stone of Masters’ Hall looking silvery in moonlight, with a single dim interior light illuminating a stained-glass window of Teccam barefoot at the mouth of his cave, speaking to students. 

 

 


What your prompt gets right
These parts are basically sound:

Masters’ Hall is a separate building

it is primarily residential

it includes masters’ quarters, resident gillers, and guest rooms

it has at least one side entrance

it is built of grey stone

it has a Teccam stained-glass window

it should feel quiet, dignified, restrained, and old rather than theatrical.

What is too strong or likely wrong
These bits need softening or removal:

1. “Residential only” is too absolute.
Canon says it contains “little more than” living quarters, resident gillers, and guest rooms. That strongly points to a residential building, but it does not justify an absolute hard ban on every other minor function like stair halls, servants’ rooms, small common rooms, or circulation spaces. 


2. “Multi-storey (at least 3–4 levels)” is not locked.
You do have a long, narrow flight of stairs, but that does not prove 3–4 storeys. It proves vertical circulation, not total floor count. 


3. “Pitched roof” is not clearly canon.
There is a roof, obviously, and a manure cart was once lifted onto the roof of Masters’ Hall, but that does not lock the roof form from the exterior. Keep roof shape reconstructed unless another evidence file freezes it. 


4. “High windows elsewhere” is unsupported.
The stained-glass window is canon. A general field of narrow vertical windows is plausible, but not text-locked from the snippets you’ve got here. Treat other windows as reconstructed, not canon. 


5. “Teccam window here, not Hollows” is strong, but be careful how you phrase it.
The moonlight passage clearly places a Teccam stained-glass window in Masters’ Hall. That means the old prompt that gave that same window to Hollows was contaminated. But it does not prove there is only one Teccam image anywhere on campus. The safe statement is: the canonical stained-glass Teccam window definitely appears in Masters’ Hall. 


6. “No classrooms, no lecture halls, no admissions spaces” is a good design guardrail, but only as reconstruction discipline.
It fits the “little more than living quarters…” line very well, but it is still better framed as “should not be treated as a teaching building” rather than a metaphysical impossibility. 


Best lore-accurate read
The most canon-safe interpretation is:

Masters’ Hall is a separate grey-stone university residence building with some internal vertical circulation, at least one side door, private or semi-private residential functions, and a stained-glass Teccam window visible from outside at night. It should read as domestic-academic, not procedural, not classroom-like, and not Hollows.

but there is a whole scene where elodin and kvothe set hemmes clothes on fire in the masters rooms

Thought for 1m 0s
Yes — and that scene actually supports a residential/private Masters’ Hall more than it weakens it.

In The Wise Man’s Fear, Kvothe explicitly says Masters’ Hall is one of the few buildings he hasn’t spent much time in because it contains “little more than living quarters for the masters, the resident gillers, and guest rooms for visiting arcanists.” Then he sees Elodin approach a side door, unlock it with a bright brass key, and lead him through a narrow stone hallway and up a long, narrow flight of stairs. A few pages later they are inside Master Hemme’s rooms, where Elodin burns Hemme’s clothes.

So the correction is:

Masters’ Hall is not just a vague dormitory shell.

It absolutely includes private masters’ rooms / suites.

It also includes enough internal circulation to reach those rooms by stair and hall.

That scene is strong evidence for a residential building with private chambers, not just a generic “quarters only” abstraction.

What it doesn’t prove is that Masters’ Hall should become a big administrative or teaching building. The same passage still frames it as containing little more than living quarters, resident gillers, and guest rooms. So the safest lore-accurate read is:

Masters’ Hall = a separate grey-stone residential building with masters’ private rooms, guest rooms, giller rooms, halls, stairs, and probably a small amount of shared domestic circulation — but not lecture halls, admissions rooms, or major public bureaucracy. 


So yes — your prompt should be updated.

The old line:

“residential only”

should become something more accurate like:

“primarily residential, with private masters’ chambers/suites and related internal circulation”

And I’d add these canon-anchored details:

side door with brass key

narrow stone hallway

long narrow stair

private master’s room / chamber exists canonically (Hemme’s rooms)

room large enough for fireplace, mantle, decanter, clothing storage, and high ceiling smoke volume 


So the cleaner verdict is:

You were right to push back.
That scene means Masters’ Hall should not be prompted as just anonymous lodging cells. It needs at least some real master suites / chambers in the reconstruction. 


If you want, I’ll rewrite the Masters’ Hall building prompt and the Masters’ Hall floor plan prompt to incorporate that scene properly.

give me best possible prompt for "Masters’ Hall site plan" please

Thought for 16s
Yes — and the big correction is this:

Your earlier “Masters’ Hall site plan” prompt was mixing two different things:

Masters’ Hall in NOTW as the formal crescent-table room Kvothe is taken to in/near Hollows for horns/admissions-era proceedings, and

Masters’ Hall in WMF as a separate residential building with masters’ quarters, guest rooms, a side door, hallway, stair, and the Teccam stained-glass window.

For a site plan prompt, the safest lore-accurate target is the WMF standalone Masters’ Hall building, because that’s the one with real exterior evidence:

separate building,

grey stone,

side door,

narrow stone hall inside,

long narrow stair,

mostly living quarters / gillers / guest rooms,

Teccam stained-glass window glowing at night.

So here’s the best possible Masters’ Hall site plan prompt I’d use:

Create a **strictly top-down, orthographic architectural site plan** of **Masters’ Hall and its immediate exterior context** from Patrick Rothfuss’s *Kingkiller Chronicle* as a **canon-constrained reconstruction**, not a fantasy invention.

This is **not** a full campus map.
This is **not** a decorative fantasy illustration.
This is **not** a courtroom, admissions theatre, or Hollows procedural room.
This is **not** a cinematic moonlit scene painting.

The image must function as a **technical planning reference** for the standalone **Masters’ Hall building**:
- a separate University residential building
- primarily private and domestic-academic in function
- containing little more than living quarters for the masters, resident gillers, and guest rooms for visiting arcanists
- quiet, old, dignified, and restrained rather than ceremonial or scholastic

## CORE INTENT
Reconstruct Masters’ Hall as faithfully as possible to the available canon while staying modest, practical, and believable as part of the University.

The image must:
- prioritize direct textual evidence first
- preserve uncertainty where evidence stops
- avoid turning Masters’ Hall into Hollows, Mains, or a lecture building
- avoid over-inventing office suites, tribunal rooms, or classrooms
- show the building as primarily residential with private circulation and chambers
- keep the design old, sober, and academically prestigious without grandeur for its own sake

## DRAWING MODE
Use:
- true top-down orthographic architectural site plan
- black-and-white planning drawing
- clear building footprint
- door positions
- paths and immediate grounds
- simple labels
- north arrow
- scale bar
- plain legend / key
- certainty categories:
  - `canon`
  - `implied`
  - `reconstructed`

Do **not** use:
- perspective
- isometric projection
- dramatic shadows
- decorative fantasy art
- heraldry
- towers added for silhouette
- theatre staging
- people
- moonlight effects as the main point of the image
- painterly atmosphere
- cinematic composition

## LOCKED CANON FACTS
Represent the following as the strongest anchors:

- Masters’ Hall is a **separate building**.
- Kvothe says it is one of the few buildings he has not spent much time in because it contains **little more than living quarters for the masters, the resident gillers, and guest rooms for visiting arcanists**.
- Elodin approaches a **side door** to Masters’ Hall.
- He unlocks that side door with a **bright brass key**.
- Inside is a **narrow stone hallway**.
- A **long, narrow flight of stairs** rises inside.
- At night, the **grey stone** of Masters’ Hall looks **silvery in moonlight**.
- A **single dim interior light** illuminates a **stained-glass window** showing **Teccam barefoot at the mouth of his cave, speaking to a crowd of young students**. 

These facts should drive the reconstruction.
Do **not** add stronger claims than these unless clearly marked `implied` or `reconstructed`.

## PRIMARY BUILDING IDENTITY
Masters’ Hall should read as:
- a standalone University residence building
- private rather than public
- academic rather than noble
- stone-built
- old, maintained, and respectable
- quieter and more domestic than Hollows
- less sprawling than Mains
- not a classroom block
- not a workshop
- not a public administrative hall

It should feel like:
- senior college lodgings
- masters’ chambers and guest suites
- restrained institutional authority
- private academic life

It should **not** feel like:
- a lecture building
- a governance palace
- a courthouse
- an admissions building
- a chapel
- a fortress
- a fantasy manor with ornamental excess

## SITE-PLAN SCOPE
Show only:
- the Masters’ Hall footprint
- immediate approach path(s)
- the side door approach
- minimal nearby university context if needed to orient the building
- subdued grounds sufficient to show access and status

Do **not** show:
- the whole University
- Hollows
- the Admissions Theatre
- the Masters’ Hall tribunal/crescent-table room from the older NOTW reading
- large unrelated neighboring buildings in detail
- decorative landscape scenes

## EXTERIOR CHARACTER
If shown in plan, the building should support:
- grey-stone construction
- restrained, institutional massing
- a side entrance with real practical use
- some plain window logic, but only conservatively
- one stained-glass window as a notable feature
- domestic-academic privacy

Do **not** overstate:
- roof form
- exact storey count
- full facade rhythm
- decorative program

These are not fully locked by canon and should remain modest.

## WINDOW LOGIC
Canon definitely supports:
- at least one stained-glass Teccam window in Masters’ Hall

Other windows may be included only conservatively as `reconstructed` or `implied`:
- plain
- practical
- non-ornamental
- consistent with a private residence building

Do **not** imply:
- cathedral glazing
- repeated stained-glass programme
- grand ceremonial fenestration

## DOOR AND ACCESS LOGIC
The site plan must make the **side door** legible.
It should feel:
- real
- used
- key-controlled
- secondary to the overall building face, not a ceremonial front portal

The plan may include a modest principal approach path and more private circulation around the sides, but do **not** invent multiple grand public entries unless clearly marked reconstructed.

## FUNCTIONAL ZONING
The site plan should imply these building functions:
- masters’ private chambers
- resident giller rooms
- guest rooms for visiting arcanists
- stair and corridor circulation
- modest service support

Keep these functions implicit through footprint and access logic.
Do not over-label the plan with many internal room names unless the image generator needs minimal internal notation.

## STRONGLY IMPLIED DESIGN LOGIC
The following are reasonable but should remain conservative and, if labelled, should be `implied` or `reconstructed`:
- modest suite or chamber hierarchy
- private residential circulation
- small internal common or landing spaces
- quieter side or rear portions for guest rooms
- simple support/service edge
- some differentiation between more senior masters’ rooms and ordinary guest/giller rooms

Do **not** let these implied functions turn the site plan into a full speculative manor diagram.

## WHAT MUST NOT BE CONTRADICTED
Absolutely do not contradict:
- Masters’ Hall is a **separate building**
- it contains **little more than living quarters, resident gillers, and guest rooms**
- the side door exists
- the brass key side-door access exists
- the narrow stone hallway exists
- the long narrow flight of stairs exists
- the Teccam stained-glass window exists
- the building is built of grey stone and reads as quiet, old, and dignified 

## HARD BANS
Do not include:
- admissions theatre elements
- crescent tribunal table
- Masters’ Hall as a room inside Hollows
- lecture halls
- classroom slates
- amphitheatres
- major public administration counters
- Archives-style severity
- Mains-style labyrinthine sprawl
- Fishery / Medica / Archives functions
- decorative towers
- fortress walls
- chapel or monastery logic
- ornate fantasy ornament
- palace forecourts

## GRAPHIC CONVENTIONS
Use:
- thick line for main exterior walls
- lighter line for secondary context
- simple labels
- plain legend:
  - `canon`
  - `implied`
  - `reconstructed`
- north arrow
- scale bar

Suggested labels:
- `Masters' Hall`
- `side door`
- `approach`
- `stained glass window`
- `grounds`
- `reconstructed residential wing` only if needed

Do **not** over-label speculative room uses on the site plan.

## UNCERTAINTY HANDLING
If a detail is unknown:
- keep it generic
- keep it modest
- keep it functional
- label it `reconstructed` where appropriate

If roof form, storey count, full window rhythm, or exact internal distribution is uncertain:
- do not pretend certainty
- keep the footprint believable and restrained
- prioritize the known side-door / residential identity over overbuilt detail

If a new detail would force contradiction:
- omit it
- simplify it
- do not improvise

## FINAL IMAGE CHARACTER
The final image should feel like:
- a private University residential building
- old, academic, and dignified
- quiet and somewhat austere
- technically useful as a planning reference
- clearly separate from Hollows and Mains

It should not feel like:
- a courtroom block
- a lecture building
- a palace
- a fortress
- a fantasy manor spectacle

## FINAL QUALITY CHECK
Before finalizing, verify:
- the building reads as standalone
- the site plan supports a side-door approach
- the building feels primarily residential
- the tone is quiet and academic
- the Teccam stained-glass window is present as a key landmark
- the plan does not drift into Hollows or admissions imagery
- uncertainty is preserved conservatively
- the image can serve as a stable reference for later Masters’ Hall floor plans

Generate **one single top-down architectural site plan image only**.

How do I turn this into a 3d video game where I can walk around all the university building up and down floors virtually as accurate to the generated floor plans as possible 

Thought for 2m 23s
Best path: Blender + Godot 4.

Why that stack:

Blender is the cleanest way to turn your floor plans into accurate 3D architecture.

Godot 4 is free, good for first-person walking, easy to structure building-by-building, and simpler than Unreal for this sort of explorable reconstruction.

If you want it to stay inside your current website later, you can export the same models to a Three.js / React Three Fiber viewer after the Godot version works.

The big rule is this:

Do not build the 3D world from the rendered PNG floor plans alone.
Build it from a canonical floor data package for each building and floor, then use the floor plan images only as visual tracing references.

The right pipeline
1. Freeze one authoritative source per floor
For each building and floor, create one structured file that contains:

outer footprint

wall lines

wall thicknesses

door positions

stair positions

window positions

room labels

certainty tags: canon / implied / reconstructed

links to source prompt / book evidence

Use something like:

json

yaml

or even svg + metadata

Example:
archives/ground_floor.json
hollows/basement.json
mains/ground_floor.json

This matters because if you change a prompt later, your 3D model should still be anchored to one stable plan.

2. Trace each floor plan in Blender
Import each approved floor plan image into Blender as a reference image on the XY plane.

Set:

1 Blender unit = 1 metre

north always aligned the same way

one collection per floor

one collection per building

Then trace:

exterior walls first

major corridors

stairs

fixed room shells

then secondary partitions

Do blockout first, not detailed modelling.

3. Standardise vertical assumptions
Your floor plans are mostly horizontal. A walkable 3D game needs vertical conventions.

Pick one project-wide vertical standard and stick to it:

public institutional floor height: around 4.0–4.8 m

ordinary upper floors: around 3.2–4.0 m

basement / utility floors: 3.0–3.8 m

stair riser: around 170 mm

stair tread: around 280 mm

door height: around 2.1–2.4 m

major hall doors can be taller

These are reconstructed conventions, not canon, so keep them in a shared config file.

4. Model each building as a stack of aligned floors
For each building:

Ground floor at Z = 0

Upper floors stacked exactly above

Basement below

Stair openings aligned vertically

Exterior walls aligned unless canon says the upper or lower floor differs

You want:

Building shell

Floor slabs

Walls

Doors

Stairs

Props

Lighting markers
as separate layers or collections

5. Keep “game mesh” and “hero mesh” separate
Make two versions:

Game mesh = simple, low-poly, clean collision

Hero mesh = nicer version for final visuals

At first, only build the game mesh.

That means:

plain walls

simple floors

simple door frames

simple stairs

no decorative clutter

no tiny books, pipes, or furniture unless needed for navigation

6. Export to Godot as GLB
Use .glb exports from Blender.

One file per building is usually best:

archives.glb

hollows.glb

mains.glb

medica.glb

Or if a building is huge:

mains_ground.glb

mains_upper.glb

How to structure the game
Recommended game structure
Make a first-person exploration game, not a traditional “game” first.

Core systems:

first-person movement

mouse look

stairs navigation

building selector

floor selector

map / compass

hotspot labels

lore notes panel

optional noclip mode for debugging

Godot scene structure
Use something like:

/project
  /scenes
    campus.tscn
    archives.tscn
    hollows.tscn
    mains.tscn
    medica.tscn
    fishery.tscn
    mews.tscn
    masters_hall.tscn
    haven.tscn
    underthing.tscn
  /models
    archives.glb
    hollows.glb
    mains.glb
    ...
  /data
    buildings.json
    floors/
    rooms/
  /scripts
    player/
    ui/
    loading/
    navigation/
Campus layout
Have one master campus scene with:

terrain / paths

major building shells

entrances

outdoor navigation

Then either:

let the player walk into buildings seamlessly, or

use doorway triggers to load interiors

For big buildings like Mains and the Underthing, doorway loading is cleaner.

What to build first
Build in this order:

Campus blockout

Archives

Hollows

Mains

Medica

Fishery / Artificery

Mews

Mess

Masters’ Hall

Haven

Underthing

Why:

campus gives you global alignment

Archives and Hollows are the cleanest controlled buildings

Mains and Underthing are the hardest, so don’t start there

What “accurate” should mean in practice
You said “as accurate to the generated floor plans as possible.”

That means you should enforce:

Accuracy layer 1
Match the approved floor plans exactly

wall locations

room proportions

stair placement

entry sequence

Accuracy layer 2
Keep cross-floor alignment consistent

stairs line up

halls don’t collide

basements stay under shells

courtyards align with roof / grate / tree logic

Accuracy layer 3
Mark uncertain things clearly
If a room or corridor is reconstructed:

add a metadata tag

optionally show a tooltip in-game:

canon

implied

reconstructed

That would make the project much stronger than a normal fan build.

Smart features to add
Must-have
Floor overlay toggle
Press a key to show the 2D floor plan under your feet.

Lore hotspot mode
Click a room to see:

room name

building

floor

certainty

book evidence

Building/floor jump menu
Jump straight to:

Archives Ground

Hollows Basement

Mains Ground

Haven Level 2

etc.

Very useful
Ghost walls / x-ray mode

Free-fly camera

North compass

Distance ruler

Day / night toggle

Legacy vs current reconstruction toggle

What Copilot in VS Code should help you build
Copilot is best used for:

scaffolding the Godot project

writing data loaders

building UI panels

creating debug tools

validating consistency

It is not the best tool for inventing the actual architecture.

Best Copilot task prompt

- Explore campus and interiors in first person

The most important technical rule
Create a file like:

{
  "building": "Archives",
  "floor": "Ground",
  "north_up": true,
  "source_plan": "archives_ground_floor_v03.png",
  "certainty": "mixed",
  "entrances": [...],
  "stairs": [...],
  "rooms": [...]
}
Then make your game read that data.

That way:

UI labels are automatic

floor toggles are automatic

hotspot text is automatic

later prompt corrections do not wreck the whole game

If you want browser-based instead of Godot
Use:

Three.js

GLTF models from Blender

That is best if you want it to live inside your current website.

But for the fastest path to a walkable explorable build:
Blender + Godot 4 is the better first move.

How do I automate this process with ai to do it least effort and time possible 

Thought for 7s
You can automate a lot of it, but not all of it.

The fastest low-effort setup is:

AI for structure, Blender for geometry, Godot for walking, scripts for glue.

Best low-effort pipeline
1. Make one structured source of truth
Stop building from loose prompts and PNGs.

Have AI turn each approved plan into a simple JSON like:

{
  "building": "Archives",
  "floor": "Ground",
  "footprint": [...],
  "walls": [...],
  "doors": [...],
  "stairs": [...],
  "rooms": [...],
  "windows": [...],
  "certainty": "mixed"
}
Do this for every building and floor.

Why:

then everything downstream can be automated

Blender import can be scripted

Godot labels and navigation can be automatic

corrections only need one change

2. Use AI to convert prompt packs into floor JSON
Use Copilot or Codex to build a parser workflow:

input: approved prompt + approved image + any notes

output: building_floor.json

Have AI generate the first draft, then you quickly check it.

This is much faster than hand-modelling from scratch.

3. Auto-generate Blender blockouts from JSON
This is the biggest time saver.

Have a Blender Python script that:

reads the floor JSON

creates wall curves or meshes

extrudes walls to standard heights

cuts doors

places stairs as simple block meshes

places floor slabs

names collections by building/floor/room

Then one click gives you a walkable blockout.

4. Auto-stack floors into buildings
A second script should:

load all floor JSON files for a building

place them at the right Z heights

align stairs

create building shells

export one .glb per building

That gets you from “2D plans” to “3D building” with very little manual effort.

5. Auto-build Godot scenes from metadata
Then use Godot scripts to:

load .glb buildings

spawn them in campus positions

generate labels from JSON

create hotspot info panels

create floor toggles

create building jump menu

create certainty overlay: canon / implied / reconstructed

So instead of hand-placing everything in Godot, the game becomes mostly data-driven.

What AI should do vs what you should still do
Let AI do
prompt cleanup

plan-to-JSON drafting

Blender script generation

Godot scene scaffolding

hotspot metadata

room labels

consistency checking

file naming / structure

batch export tools

You still do
approve the canonical floor data

fix weird stair geometry

check proportions

decide heights where canon is silent

visually inspect walkability

reject bad AI assumptions

That’s the real balance.

Least-effort automation stack
Option A — best practical stack

Best Copilot prompt for this
Paste this into VS Code Copilot:

I want to automate a data-driven pipeline for turning approved Kingkiller Chronicle floor plans into a walkable 3D Godot project.

Build this in stages:

1. Define a JSON schema for a building floor plan with:
- building
- floor
- footprint
- walls
- doors
- stairs
- windows
- rooms
- labels
- certainty tags (canon, implied, reconstructed)
- metadata links to source prompt/image

2. Write a Python tool that reads those JSON files and validates:
- closed wall loops
- stairs alignment between floors
- room containment inside footprint
- duplicate IDs
- missing labels

3. Write a Blender Python script that:
- imports one floor JSON
- creates floor slab
- extrudes walls
- cuts door openings
- places simple stairs
- creates collections by building/floor
- names meshes cleanly

4. Write a second Blender script that:
- stacks all floors for a building vertically
- aligns stairs
- exports one GLB per building

5. Write a Godot 4 loader that:
- loads building GLBs
- places them on a campus map
- supports first-person walking
- supports floor visibility toggles
- supports hotspot metadata panels
- supports building/floor jump menu

6. Keep everything modular and easy to expand building-by-building.

Important:
- Prefer simple walkable blockouts over pretty meshes
- Assume one Blender unit = one metre
- Assume north must stay consistent
- Preserve uncertainty tags in metadata
- Do not hardcode room names outside the JSON

Best path: Blender + Godot 4.
Why that stack:
Blender is the cleanest way to turn your floor plans into accurate 3D architecture.
Godot 4 is free, good for first-person walking, easy to structure building-by-building, and simpler than Unreal for this sort of explorable reconstruction.
If you want it to stay inside your current website later, you can export the same models to a Three.js / React Three Fiber viewer after the Godot version works.
The big rule is this:
Do not build the 3D world from the rendered PNG floor plans alone.
 Build it from a canonical floor data package for each building and floor, then use the floor plan images only as visual tracing references.
The right pipeline
1. Freeze one authoritative source per floor
For each building and floor, create one structured file that contains:
outer footprint
wall lines
wall thicknesses
door positions
stair positions
window positions
room labels
certainty tags: canon / implied / reconstructed
links to source prompt / book evidence
Use something like:
json
yaml
or even svg + metadata
Example: archives/ground_floor.json hollows/basement.json mains/ground_floor.json
This matters because if you change a prompt later, your 3D model should still be anchored to one stable plan.
2. Trace each floor plan in Blender
Import each approved floor plan image into Blender as a reference image on the XY plane.
Set:
1 Blender unit = 1 metre
north always aligned the same way
one collection per floor
one collection per building
Then trace:
exterior walls first
major corridors
stairs
fixed room shells
then secondary partitions
Do blockout first, not detailed modelling.
3. Standardise vertical assumptions
Your floor plans are mostly horizontal. A walkable 3D game needs vertical conventions.
Pick one project-wide vertical standard and stick to it:
public institutional floor height: around 4.0–4.8 m
ordinary upper floors: around 3.2–4.0 m
basement / utility floors: 3.0–3.8 m
stair riser: around 170 mm
stair tread: around 280 mm
door height: around 2.1–2.4 m
major hall doors can be taller
These are reconstructed conventions, not canon, so keep them in a shared config file.
4. Model each building as a stack of aligned floors
For each building:
Ground floor at Z = 0
Upper floors stacked exactly above
Basement below
Stair openings aligned vertically
Exterior walls aligned unless canon says the upper or lower floor differs
You want:
Building shell
Floor slabs
Walls
Doors
Stairs
Props
Lighting markers as separate layers or collections
5. Keep “game mesh” and “hero mesh” separate
Make two versions:
Game mesh = simple, low-poly, clean collision
Hero mesh = nicer version for final visuals
At first, only build the game mesh.
That means:
plain walls
simple floors
simple door frames
simple stairs
no decorative clutter
no tiny books, pipes, or furniture unless needed for navigation
6. Export to Godot as GLB
Use .glb exports from Blender.
One file per building is usually best:
archives.glb
hollows.glb
mains.glb
medica.glb
Or if a building is huge:
mains_ground.glb
mains_upper.glb
How to structure the game
Recommended game structure
Make a first-person exploration game, not a traditional “game” first.
Core systems:
first-person movement
mouse look
stairs navigation
building selector
floor selector
map / compass
hotspot labels
lore notes panel
optional noclip mode for debugging
Godot scene structure
Use something like:
/project
  /scenes
    campus.tscn
    archives.tscn
    hollows.tscn
    mains.tscn
    medica.tscn
    fishery.tscn
    mews.tscn
    masters_hall.tscn
    haven.tscn
    underthing.tscn
  /models
    archives.glb
    hollows.glb
    mains.glb
    ...
  /data
    buildings.json
    floors/
    rooms/
  /scripts
    player/
    ui/
    loading/
    navigation/

Campus layout
Have one master campus scene with:
terrain / paths
major building shells
entrances
outdoor navigation
Then either:
let the player walk into buildings seamlessly, or
use doorway triggers to load interiors
For big buildings like Mains and the Underthing, doorway loading is cleaner.
What to build first
Build in this order:
Campus blockout
Archives
Hollows
Mains
Medica
Fishery / Artificery
Mews
Mess
Masters’ Hall
Haven
Underthing
Why:
campus gives you global alignment
Archives and Hollows are the cleanest controlled buildings
Mains and Underthing are the hardest, so don’t start there
What “accurate” should mean in practice
You said “as accurate to the generated floor plans as possible.”
That means you should enforce:
Accuracy layer 1
Match the approved floor plans exactly
wall locations
room proportions
stair placement
entry sequence
Accuracy layer 2
Keep cross-floor alignment consistent
stairs line up
halls don’t collide
basements stay under shells
courtyards align with roof / grate / tree logic
Accuracy layer 3
Mark uncertain things clearly If a room or corridor is reconstructed:
add a metadata tag
optionally show a tooltip in-game:
canon
implied
reconstructed
That would make the project much stronger than a normal fan build.

 Jump straight to:

What Copilot in VS Code should help you build
Copilot is best used for:
scaffolding the Godot project
writing data loaders
building UI panels
creating debug tools
validating consistency
It is not the best tool for inventing the actual architecture.

Help me build a Godot 4 first-person exploration project for a canon-constrained 3D reconstruction of the University from the Kingkiller Chronicle.

Project goals:
- Explore campus and interiors in first person
- Walk up and down floors
- Load building models exported from Blender as .glb
- Keep building/floor metadata in JSON
- Support room labels, building labels, and certainty tags: canon, implied, reconstructed

  - building selector
  - floor selector
  - minimap / compass
  - hotspot info panel

Please scaffold:
1. scene structure
2. player controller
3. building loader
4. floor visibility manager
5. hotspot metadata system
6. UI panels
7. JSON schema for buildings/floors/rooms
8. debug tools for checking stair alignment and room overlap

Assume:
- models are authored in Blender
- one Blender unit = one metre

The most important technical rule
Create a file like:
{
  "building": "Archives",
  "floor": "Ground",
  "north_up": true,
  "source_plan": "archives_ground_floor_v03.png",
  "certainty": "mixed",
  "entrances": [...],
  "stairs": [...],
  "rooms": [...]
}

Then make your game read that data.
That way:
UI labels are automatic
floor toggles are automatic
hotspot text is automatic
later prompt corrections do not wreck the whole game
If you want browser-based instead of Godot
Use:
React Three Fiber
Three.js
GLTF models from Blender
That is best if you want it to live inside your current website.
But for the fastest path to a walkable explorable build: Blender + Godot 4 is the better first move.

add building/floor selector
walk around Archives and Hollows first

You can automate a lot of it, but not all of it.
The fastest low-effort setup is:
AI for structure, Blender for geometry, Godot for walking, scripts for glue.
Best low-effort pipeline
1. Make one structured source of truth
Stop building from loose prompts and PNGs.
Have AI turn each approved plan into a simple JSON like:
{
  "building": "Archives",
  "floor": "Ground",
  "footprint": [...],
  "walls": [...],
  "doors": [...],
  "stairs": [...],
  "rooms": [...],
  "windows": [...],
  "certainty": "mixed"
}

Do this for every building and floor.
Why:
then everything downstream can be automated
Blender import can be scripted
Godot labels and navigation can be automatic
corrections only need one change
2. Use AI to convert prompt packs into floor JSON
Use Copilot or Codex to build a parser workflow:
input: approved prompt + approved image + any notes
output: building_floor.json
Have AI generate the first draft, then you quickly check it.
This is much faster than hand-modelling from scratch.
3. Auto-generate Blender blockouts from JSON
This is the biggest time saver.
Have a Blender Python script that:
reads the floor JSON
creates wall curves or meshes
extrudes walls to standard heights
cuts doors
places stairs as simple block meshes
places floor slabs
names collections by building/floor/room
Then one click gives you a walkable blockout.
4. Auto-stack floors into buildings
A second script should:
load all floor JSON files for a building
place them at the right Z heights
align stairs
create building shells
export one .glb per building
That gets you from “2D plans” to “3D building” with very little manual effort.
5. Auto-build Godot scenes from metadata
Then use Godot scripts to:
load .glb buildings
spawn them in campus positions
generate labels from JSON
create hotspot info panels
create floor toggles
create building jump menu
create certainty overlay: canon / implied / reconstructed
So instead of hand-placing everything in Godot, the game becomes mostly data-driven.

What AI should do vs what you should still do
Let AI do
prompt cleanup
plan-to-JSON drafting
Blender script generation
Godot scene scaffolding
hotspot metadata
room labels
consistency checking
file naming / structure
batch export tools
You still do
approve the canonical floor data
fix weird stair geometry
check proportions
decide heights where canon is silent
visually inspect walkability
reject bad AI assumptions
That’s the real balance.

Transforming these "canon-constrained" reconstructions into a walkable 3D environment is a massive undertaking, but honestly, it’s the ultimate dream for any *Kingkiller* fan. Since you have the floor plans, you have the "blueprint" layer finished. Now you need to move into the **Digital Stonework** phase.
Here is the roadmap to turning these 2D plans into a 1:1 virtual University.
## 1. Pick Your Engine
For a project focusing on atmospheric lighting and interior "vibes," you have two main contenders:
 * **Unreal Engine 5 (UE5):** Best for **Atmosphere**. Its "Lumen" lighting system is perfect for those unwavering sympathy lamps and the silvery moonlight on the Masters' Hall. It handles massive architectural detail very well.
 * **Unity:** Best for **Accessibility**. If you’re new to coding, Unity has a massive library of "First Person Controller" assets that will get you walking in minutes.
## 2. The "Blueprint to Bricks" Workflow
You can't just drop a 2D image into a game and expect it to have collisions. You need to **extrude** the geometry.
### Step A: Reference Planes
Import the floor plan images as a "Plane" in a 3D modeling tool like **Blender** or directly into the engine using a tool like **ProBuilder** (Unity) or **Modeling Mode** (Unreal). Scale the plane so the 10m scale bar on the image matches 10 units in the engine.
### Step B: Blocking Out (Greyboxing)
Trace the walls. Don't worry about textures yet. Just create simple boxes where the walls are.
 * **Archives:** Focus on the thick, windowless outer shell first.
 * **Mains:** This will be your hardest task because of the "lichen-like" growth. You'll need to vary the heights of different "absorbed" building sections.
## 3. Mastering Verticality
Accuracy depends on how the floors stack.
 * **Vertical Alignment:** Create a "Master Stairwell" object. In the Archives, align the **Southeast Stairwell** across all 6 floors. If the stairs don't line up perfectly due to AI generation quirks, you’ll have to "nudge" the floor plans until the stairs create a continuous path.
 * **The Underthing:** This should be its own "level" or a massive basement. Use the "Drainage Grate" in the **Hidden Apple-Tree Courtyard** of Mains as your vertical anchor.
## 4. Canon-Accurate Lighting
Lighting is what makes the University feel real. You’ll want to use **Baked Lighting** for the Archives and **Dynamic Lighting** for areas with "magic."
 * **Sympathy Lamps:** Place small, point-light sources with a slightly reddish/warm hue in the Archives and Tomes.
 * **The "Circle of Light":** In the Admissions Theatre, use a **Spotlight** with a sharp falloff to create that interrogation feel.
 * **The Stacks:** Keep the ambient light at near-zero. Force the player to use a "Hand Lamp" (a point light attached to the player's camera) to see the shelving labels.
## 5. Interaction and "Feel"
Walking is easy; interacting is where the lore comes alive.
| Feature | Implementation |
|---|---|
| **Heavy Doors** | Use "Physics Constraints." The stone doors of the Archives should feel heavy and slow to open. |
| **The "Sticky" Door** | For Elodin's Naming Hall, write a simple script that requires the player to "Interact" 3 times before the door successfully opens. |
| **Lockpicking** | In Hollows, you can implement a simple UI mini-game to simulate Kvothe picking the two locks to get into the observation area. |
## 6. Dealing with "The Labyrinth" (Mains)
Since Mains is an "acre and a half" of nonsense, it will be your biggest performance hurdle.
 * **Level Streaming:** Only load the wing the player is currently in.
 * **NavMesh:** You'll need to generate a "Navigation Mesh" so the engine knows where the floor is. Because Mains has "stairs to nowhere," you might find your character getting stuck in "bricked-off" rooms. You’ll have to manually "clip" these areas so the player can't phase through the walls into the void.
### Pro-Tip: The "Blender" Shortcut
If you want to be *truly* accurate, I recommend using **Blender** to build the 3D shells first. Trace the floor plans, "E" (Extrude) the walls up to a standard 3-4 meter height, and then export that as an .FBX file into your game engine.
**Would you like to start with a specific building? I can break down the "Room Heights" and "Ceiling Logic" for the Archives or Mains to help you stack them!**

To do this with "least effort," you have to stop thinking like an architect and start thinking like a **systems integrator**. You want to use a pipeline where one AI interprets the plan and another AI builds the geometry.
Here is the "Lazy Path" to a walkable University, ranked by speed and automation.
## 1. AI Image-to-3D Extrusion (The 5-Minute Method)
The fastest way to get walls is to use a dedicated **Floor Plan to 3D** service. These are usually designed for real estate, but they work for "Tomes" or "The Medica" if the lines are clean.
 * **Tools:** **CubiCasa**, **Planner 5D**, or **Canvas (by Occipital)**.
 * **Process:** 1.  Upload the generated floor plan image.
   2.  The AI identifies "walls," "doors," and "openings."
   3.  Export as an .OBJ or .FBX.
 * **Downside:** These tools struggle with the "Underthing" or "Mains" because they expect 90-degree residential walls. They might try to turn a "Runic Grate" into a window.
## 2. The "Blender Scripting" Shortcut (The Pro-Automation Path)
Since you already have clean black-and-white

Inspect local rendering problems, framerate issues, collision bugs, or shader issues on your hardware.

Use agentic coding tools to repeatedly modify, run, test, and patch your codebase in a tight loop.

Batch-process dozens of your local files directly unless you upload them here.

Integrate with your exact file paths, local plugins, and VS Code workspace settings without access to them.

Do visual in-editor tasks like:

repositioning stairs

checking ceiling clearance

fixing navigation meshes

adjusting collisions

tweaking camera feel

Turn your current files into a full playable game automatically without some external execution environment.

Handle long-running automated pipelines on your computer while you are away, unless another tool/service is doing that.

3. Things only a human can actually do
Decide which reconstruction choices you are willing to canonically accept when the books are ambiguous.

Choose whether a result is “good enough” artistically, emotionally, and aesthetically.

Judge whether the project still feels like Rothfuss’s University rather than a technically correct but soulless layout.

Make final calls when two plausible reconstructions conflict.

Notice when something “feels wrong” even if all the metadata looks correct.

Decide how much uncertainty you want to expose versus smooth over for users.

Prioritise your own time, energy, and scope so the project stays sustainable.

Decide what version of the project matters most:

pure scholarship

explorable fan experience

    "id", "building", "floor", "north_up", "units", "story_role",
    "source_plan", "certainty_summary", "footprint", "walls", "doors",
    "stairs", "rooms", "labels", "defaults", "levels"
  ],
  "properties": {
    "id": {"type": "string"},
    "building": {"type": "string"},
    "floor": {"type": "string"},
    "story_role": {
      "type": "string",
      "enum": ["basement", "ground", "upper", "roof", "sublevel", "mezzanine"]
    },

    "units": {"type": "string", "enum": ["m"]},
    "source_plan": {
      "type": "object",
      "required": ["image", "prompt_ref", "notes"],

# 3. Blender importer skeleton
blender_importer = r'''
import json
import math
from pathlib import Path
import bpy
from mathutils import Vector

JSON_PATH = Path("/ABSOLUTE/PATH/TO/archives_ground.json")

def clear_scene():
    bpy.ops.object.select_all(action='SELECT')
    bpy.ops.object.delete(use_global=False)

def ensure_collection(name, parent=None):
    if name in bpy.data.collections:
        return bpy.data.collections[name]
    col = bpy.data.collections.new(name)
    if parent is None:
        bpy.context.scene.collection.children.link(col)
    else:
        parent.children.link(col)
    return col

def load_data(path):
    return json.loads(Path(path).read_text())

def point_lookup(data):
    return {p["id"]: Vector((p["x"], p["y"], 0.0)) for p in data.get("points", [])}

def make_floor_slab(name, footprint, z, thickness, collection):
    mesh = bpy.data.meshes.new(name + "_mesh")
    obj = bpy.data.objects.new(name, mesh)
    collection.objects.link(obj)
    verts = [(p["x"], p["y"], z) for p in footprint]
    faces = [tuple(range(len(verts)))]
    mesh.from_pydata(verts, [], faces)
    mesh.update()
    bpy.context.view_layer.objects.active = obj
    obj.select_set(True)
    bpy.ops.object.mode_set(mode='EDIT')
    bpy.ops.mesh.extrude_region_move(TRANSFORM_OT_translate={"value": (0, 0, thickness)})
    bpy.ops.object.mode_set(mode='OBJECT')
    obj.select_set(False)
    return obj

def build_wall_segment(name, a, b, thickness, height, z, collection):
    direction = (b - a)
    length = direction.length
    center = (a + b) / 2
    bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(location=(center.x, center.y, z + height / 2))
    obj = bpy.context.active_object
    obj.name = name
    obj.scale = (length / 2, thickness / 2, height / 2)
    angle = math.atan2(direction.y, direction.x)
    obj.rotation_euler[2] = angle
    collection.objects.link(obj)
    bpy.context.scene.collection.objects.unlink(obj)
    return obj

def build_walls(data, collection):
    pts = point_lookup(data)
    defaults = data["defaults"]
    z = data["levels"]["floor_z"] + data["levels"]["slab_thickness"]
    for wall in data["walls"]:
        a = pts[wall["a"]]
        b = pts[wall["b"]]
        thickness = wall.get("thickness", defaults["wall_thickness"])
        build_wall_segment(wall["id"], a, b, thickness, defaults["wall_height"], z, collection)

def build_room_markers(data, collection):
    for room in data["rooms"]:
        poly = room["polygon"]
        xs = [p["x"] for p in poly]
        ys = [p["y"] for p in poly]
        cx = sum(xs) / len(xs)
        cy = sum(ys) / len(ys)
        bpy.ops.object.empty_add(type='PLAIN_AXES', location=(cx, cy, data["levels"]["floor_z"] + 0.2))
        obj = bpy.context.active_object
        obj.name = f'ROOM_{room["id"]}_{room["name"]}'
        collection.objects.link(obj)
        bpy.context.scene.collection.objects.unlink(obj)

def build_stairs(data, collection):
    z = data["levels"]["floor_z"] + data["levels"]["slab_thickness"]
    for stair in data["stairs"]:
        bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(location=(stair["x"], stair["y"], z + 0.15))
        obj = bpy.context.active_object
        obj.name = stair["id"]
        obj.scale = (stair["run_length"]/2, stair["width"]/2, 0.15)
        collection.objects.link(obj)
        bpy.context.scene.collection.objects.unlink(obj)

def main():
    clear_scene()
    data = load_data(JSON_PATH)
    building_col = ensure_collection(data["building"])
    floor_col = ensure_collection(data["floor"], building_col)
    make_floor_slab(f'{data["building"]}_{data["floor"]}_slab',
                    data["footprint"],
                    data["levels"]["floor_z"],
                    data["levels"]["slab_thickness"],
                    floor_col)
    build_walls(data, floor_col)
    build_stairs(data, floor_col)
    build_room_markers(data, floor_col)
    print(f'Imported {data["id"]}')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
'''
(base / 'blender').mkdir(exist_ok=True)
(base / 'blender' / 'import_floor_json.py').write_text(textwrap.dedent(blender_importer).strip() + "\n")

# 4. Blender stacker/exporter
blender_stacker = r'''
import json
from pathlib import Path
import bpy

FLOOR_DIR = Path("/ABSOLUTE/PATH/TO/floors")
EXPORT_DIR = Path("/ABSOLUTE/PATH/TO/models")
BUILDING_NAME = "Archives"

def load_floor_files(building_name):
    files = sorted(FLOOR_DIR.glob("*.json"))
    return [f for f in files if json.loads(f.read_text()).get("building") == building_name]

def import_script(path_to_script):
    with open(path_to_script, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        code = compile(f.read(), str(path_to_script), 'exec')
        exec(code, globals())

def export_glb(building_name):
    EXPORT_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    filepath = EXPORT_DIR / f"{building_name.lower()}.glb"
    bpy.ops.export_scene.gltf(filepath=str(filepath), export_format='GLB')
    print(f"Exported {filepath}")

# Intended use:
# 1. Set each JSON_PATH in import script
# 2. Import each floor JSON into same scene
# 3. Verify vertical alignment
# 4. Export GLB
'''
(base / 'blender' / 'build_and_export.py').write_text(textwrap.dedent(blender_stacker).strip() + "\n")

# 5. Validator
validator = r'''
import json
from pathlib import Path
from collections import defaultdict

FLOOR_DIR = Path("./examples")

def load_jsons():
    return [json.loads(p.read_text()) for p in FLOOR_DIR.glob("*.json")]

def validate_closed_footprint(data):
    pts = data["footprint"]
    return len(pts) >= 3

def validate_ids(data):
    seen = set()
    problems = []
    for group in ["walls", "doors", "stairs", "rooms", "hotspots"]:
        for item in data.get(group, []):
            item_id = item["id"]
            if item_id in seen:
                problems.append(f"Duplicate id: {item_id}")
            seen.add(item_id)
    return problems

def validate_door_wall_refs(data):
    wall_ids = {w["id"] for w in data["walls"]}
    problems = []
    for d in data["doors"]:
        if d["wall_id"] not in wall_ids:
            problems.append(f'Door {d["id"]} references missing wall {d["wall_id"]}')
    return problems

def validate_stair_alignment(datas):
    by_building = defaultdict(list)
    for d in datas:
        by_building[d["building"]].append(d)
    problems = []
    for building, floors in by_building.items():
        names = defaultdict(list)
        for f in floors:
            for s in f.get("stairs", []):
                names[s["name"]].append((f["floor"], s["x"], s["y"]))
        for stair_name, uses in names.items():
            xs = {round(u[1], 2) for u in uses}
            ys = {round(u[2], 2) for u in uses}
            if len(uses) > 1 and (len(xs) > 1 or len(ys) > 1):
                problems.append(f"{building}: stair '{stair_name}' misaligned across floors: {uses}")
    return problems

def main():
    datas = load_jsons()
    problems = []
    for d in datas:
        if not validate_closed_footprint(d):
            problems.append(f'{d["id"]}: invalid footprint')
        problems.extend([f'{d["id"]}: {p}' for p in validate_ids(d)])
        problems.extend([f'{d["id"]}: {p}' for p in validate_door_wall_refs(d)])
    problems.extend(validate_stair_alignment(datas))
    if problems:
        print("VALIDATION PROBLEMS:")
        for p in problems:
            print("-", p)
    else:
        print("All validations passed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
'''
(base / 'tools').mkdir(exist_ok=True)
(base / 'tools' / 'validate_floor_data.py').write_text(textwrap.dedent(validator).strip() + "\n")

# 6. Godot loader/data schema docs
godot_readme = """
# Godot loader notes

Expected folder layout:

godot/
  data/
    buildings.json
    floors/
  scenes/
  scripts/

`buildings.json` example:
{
  "campus_north_up": true,
  "buildings": [
    {
      "id": "archives",
      "name": "Archives",
      "glb": "res://models/archives.glb",
      "position": [0, 0, 0],
      "rotation_deg": 0,
      "floors": [
        {"id": "archives_ground_v01", "label": "Ground", "json": "res://data/floors/archives_ground.json"}
      ]
    }
  ]
}
"""
(base / 'godot').mkdir(exist_ok=True)
(base / 'godot' / 'README.md').write_text(textwrap.dedent(godot_readme).strip() + "\n")

godot_loader = r'''
extends Node3D

@export var buildings_manifest_path: String = "res://data/buildings.json"

func _ready() -> void:
	var data = _load_json(buildings_manifest_path)
	if data.is_empty():
		push_error("No building manifest loaded.")
		return

	for building in data.get("buildings", []):
		_spawn_building(building)

func _load_json(path: String) -> Dictionary:
	if not FileAccess.file_exists(path):
		return {}
	var f = FileAccess.open(path, FileAccess.READ)
	var text = f.get_as_text()
	var parsed = JSON.parse_string(text)
	if typeof(parsed) == TYPE_DICTIONARY:
		return parsed
	return {}

func _spawn_building(building: Dictionary) -> void:
	var glb_path = building.get("glb", "")
	if glb_path == "":
		return

	var scene_res = load(glb_path)
	if scene_res == null:
		push_warning("Could not load GLB: %s" % glb_path)
		return

	var inst = scene_res.instantiate()
	inst.name = building.get("name", "Building")
	var pos = building.get("position", [0,0,0])
	inst.position = Vector3(pos[0], pos[1], pos[2])
	inst.rotation_degrees = Vector3(0, building.get("rotation_deg", 0), 0)
	add_child(inst)
'''
(base / 'godot' / 'building_loader.gd').write_text(textwrap.dedent(godot_loader).strip() + "\n")

extends Area3D

@export var title: String = ""
@export var building: String = ""

@export_multiline var lore_text: String = ""

signal hotspot_focused(data: Dictionary)

func interact() -> void:
	emit_signal("hotspot_focused", {

godot_player = r'''
extends CharacterBody3D

@export var speed := 5.0
@export var mouse_sensitivity := 0.002
@onready var head: Node3D = $Head
@onready var camera: Camera3D = $Head/Camera3D

func _ready() -> void:
	Input.set_mouse_mode(Input.MOUSE_MODE_CAPTURED)

func _unhandled_input(event: InputEvent) -> void:
	if event is InputEventMouseMotion:
		rotate_y(-event.relative.x * mouse_sensitivity)
		head.rotate_x(-event.relative.y * mouse_sensitivity)
		head.rotation.x = clamp(head.rotation.x, deg_to_rad(-89), deg_to_rad(89))

func _physics_process(delta: float) -> void:
	var input_dir := Input.get_vector("move_left", "move_right", "move_forward", "move_back")
	var basis := global_transform.basis
	var dir := (basis.z * input_dir.y + basis.x * input_dir.x).normalized()
	velocity.x = dir.x * speed
	velocity.z = dir.z * speed
	if not is_on_floor():
		velocity += get_gravity() * delta
	move_and_slide()
'''
(base / 'godot' / 'first_person_player.gd').write_text(textwrap.dedent(godot_player).strip() + "\n")

    "id": {"type":"string"},
    "building": {"type":"string"},
    "floor": {"type":"string"},
